Most DORA dashboards start from the same raw material: your Git history, your tags and your pipeline events. We wanted to know how much of the final number comes from that data and how much comes from the definitions the tool picks for you. So we computed the four DORA metrics for one year of four open-source projects, under each definition we see in scripts and tools, and placed every result in the performance levels of the 2024 DORA report.

The definition alone moved every project across at least two levels. From the same Kubernetes history, deployment frequency is **Medium** (42 final releases, 0.81 a week), **High** (63 tags with the alpha, beta and rc tags, 1.21 a week) or **Elite** (3,286 landings on master, 63 a week). Lead time is **High** (5.2 days) if a landing on master counts as the deploy, **Medium** (25.9 days) to the first tag, and **Low** (76.6 days) to the first final release. For uv, which squash-merges, the lead time from first commit to landing is 0.0 hours, because its squash commits keep no record of when the work started.

## TL;DR

- **A deploy is a definition.** Every project lands in **Elite** for deployment frequency if each landing on the default branch counts, and in **Medium** or **High** if releases count.
- **Lead time depends on where the clock starts and stops.** Kubernetes ranges from 5.2 days to 81.9 days on the same history, depending on the start timestamp, the event that stops the clock, and whether backports count.
- **The merge button can erase history.** In 3,090 of uv's 3,132 landings, the author date is within a minute of the landing time. Prometheus uses both buttons: changes merged with a merge commit show 48.2 hours from first commit to merge, squashed changes show 0.0 hours.
- **Change failure rate is the least stable of the four.** uv is at 86.1%, 19.8% or 5.0% depending on whether a failure is a quick patch release, any revert, or a revert of a change that shipped.
- **What to do:** write the definitions down, keep them fixed, and compare a team with its own past, not with a level chart or another tool.

## Prerequisites

- `git` and `python3` (standard library only), plus `bc` for the merge-style demo
- A repository whose tags mark releases with version numbers
- The companion repo [dora-from-git](https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git), which holds every script and every recorded result in this post

## How we measured

We took one year, 1 October 2025 to 1 October 2026, and four projects that merge in different ways:

| Repo                            | How changes land                                                    | What marks a release                        |
| ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| kubernetes/kubernetes           | merge commits                                                       | `vX.Y.Z` tags, plus alpha, beta and rc tags |
| astral-sh/uv                    | squash                                                              | `X.Y.Z` tags                                |
| golang/go (go.googlesource.com) | rebase through Gerrit, almost linear history                        | `goX.Y.Z` tags, plus beta and rc tags       |
| prometheus/prometheus           | mixed: 626 merge commits and 615 squashed or pushed commits on main | `vX.Y.Z` tags, plus rc tags                 |

The scripts use plain Git and nothing else. Go is hosted on Gerrit, not GitHub, and the same code reads it. Treeless clones keep every commit and tag but no file contents, which is enough for metadata: the four repos take 132 MB, and a full run took 137 seconds on a Raspberry Pi 4 (`results/benchmark.txt`).

The pipeline is four stages, each writing a file the next one reads:

1. `00-clone.sh` clones each repo and records its head commit.
2. `01-extract.py` records, for every tag in the window, the commits it shipped for the first time, every first-parent commit on the default branch with the commits it brought in, and every revert with the commit or Gerrit change its message names.
3. `02-metrics.py` computes each metric under each definition.
4. `03-tiers.py` places the results in the 2024 levels.

Before we trusted the extract, `check-method.py` compared it with plain Git on seeded random samples. The commits each merge brought in matched `git rev-list --no-merges <first-parent>..<merge>` on 58 of 58 merges, and the first final release that shipped a commit matched `git tag --contains` on 60 of 60 commits.

The levels come from the [2024 Accelerate State of DevOps report](https://dora.dev/research/2024/dora-report/) (version 2024.3, page 13):

| Level  | Change lead time                 | Deployment frequency                             | Change fail rate | Failed deployment recovery time |
| ------ | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ | ---------------- | ------------------------------- |
| Elite  | Less than one day                | On demand (multiple deploys per day)             | 5%               | Less than one hour              |
| High   | Between one day and one week     | Between once per day and once per week           | 20%              | Less than one day               |
| Medium | Between one week and one month   | Between once per week and once per month         | 10%              | Less than one day               |
| Low    | Between one month and six months | Between once per month and once every six months | 40%              | Between one week and one month  |

The report describes these as clusters of survey answers, not as thresholds. We use the ranges the way a dashboard does, as bands. We place only deployment frequency and lead time, because change fail rate is a single value per level and is not in order: Medium (10%) sits below High (20%).

:::note
These projects ship releases, not production deploys, so a tag stands in for a deploy. That makes them a fair test of the definitions, and an unfair basis for ranking the projects. Nothing here says one project is better run than another.
:::

## What counts as a deploy

Four common definitions: every tag, every final release, every day with a final release, and every landing on the default branch (a merge, a squash or a direct commit). A tool that reads only the Git host sees no deploys, so the last one is the event it can count.

| Repo       | Every tag         | Final releases    | Release days      | Landings on default branch |
| ---------- | ----------------- | ----------------- | ----------------- | -------------------------- |
| Kubernetes | 1.21/week, High   | 0.81/week, Medium | 0.29/week, Medium | 63.0/week, Elite           |
| uv         | 1.94/week, High   | 1.94/week, High   | 1.78/week, High   | 60.1/week, Elite           |
| Go         | 0.69/week, Medium | 0.58/week, Medium | 0.29/week, Medium | 65.6/week, Elite           |
| Prometheus | 0.75/week, Medium | 0.50/week, Medium | 0.46/week, Medium | 23.8/week, Elite           |

Kubernetes shows how much the choice matters. It cut 42 final releases in the year, but only on 15 days, because it patches several release lines on the same day. Count tags, releases or release days and you get three different numbers from the same calendar. Count landings and every project is Elite, including Go, whose master branch is not a release.

Your company has the same choice, with different names: every merge to main, every pipeline run, every rollout to production, or every release train. Pick one and write it down.

## Where the lead time clock starts and stops

Lead time has two ends, and Git offers several timestamps for each.

```diagram
{
  "type": "flow",
  "nodes": [
    { "label": "First commit", "sub": "author date", "icon": "branch", "tone": "slate" },
    { "label": "Last rebase or amend", "sub": "committer date", "icon": "gear", "tone": "slate" },
    { "label": "Lands on main", "sub": "merge or submit", "icon": "check", "tone": "blue" },
    { "label": "First tag", "sub": "alpha, beta or rc", "icon": "box", "tone": "amber" },
    { "label": "Final release", "sub": "the deploy", "icon": "rocket", "tone": "green" }
  ]
}
```

Here is Kubernetes, from the same history, seven ways. The rows do not cover identical sets of changes: a landing-based row counts what landed in the window, a release-based row counts what releases in the window shipped, and each stop event picks up a slightly different set.

| Clock starts                           | Clock stops                              | Changes | Median | Level  |
| -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------: | -----: | ------ |
| first commit of the change             | landing on master, if that is the deploy |   3,286 |  5.2 d | High   |
| commit author date                     | first tag, alpha to rc included          |   6,812 | 25.9 d | Medium |
| landing on master                      | first final release                      |   3,734 | 60.6 d | Low    |
| commit committer date                  | first final release                      |   6,091 | 63.1 d | Low    |
| commit author date                     | first final release                      |   6,091 | 76.6 d | Low    |
| first commit of the change             | first final release                      |   3,734 | 77.0 d | Low    |
| commit author date, backports left out | first final release                      |   5,486 | 81.9 d | Low    |

```chart
{
  "type": "bar",
  "title": "Kubernetes change lead time, same history, seven definitions",
  "unit": " days",
  "caption": "Median days. 1 Oct 2025 to 1 Oct 2026. Source: results/summary.md in The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git.",
  "rows": [
    { "label": "Landing is the deploy", "value": 5.2, "series": "High" },
    { "label": "To first tag (alpha to rc)", "value": 25.9, "series": "Medium" },
    { "label": "Landing to final release", "value": 60.6, "series": "Low" },
    { "label": "Committer date to final", "value": 63.1, "series": "Low" },
    { "label": "Author date to final", "value": 76.6, "series": "Low" },
    { "label": "First commit of change to final", "value": 77.0, "series": "Low" },
    { "label": "Author date, no backports", "value": 81.9, "series": "Low" }
  ],
  "series": [
    { "name": "High", "color": "#10b981" },
    { "name": "Medium", "color": "#f59e0b" },
    { "name": "Low", "color": "#ef4444" }
  ]
}
```

Four choices do most of the moving:

- **Which release stops the clock.** Kubernetes cuts alpha tags from master, so a commit reaches its first tag 25.9 days after it is written, and its first final release 76.6 days after. If your release candidates never serve users, they are not deploys.
- **Author date or committer date.** The committer date changes every time a commit is rebased or amended, the author date does not. On Kubernetes, switching to the committer date moves the median from 76.6 to 63.1 days.
- **Backports.** For 605 of the 6,091 commits that Kubernetes final releases shipped, we found no landing on master between October 2024 and October 2026. They were made on release branches: mostly cherry-picked fixes, plus the release commits themselves. `git cherry-pick` keeps the original author date, so a cherry-pick counts the time since the fix was first written. Leaving these commits out moves the median from 76.6 to 81.9 days.
- **Per commit or per change.** We count a change as one landing on the default branch, so a pull request merged with a merge commit counts once, while a rebased pull request with ten commits counts ten times, and a commit with no landing on master counts on its own, from its commit date. On Kubernetes, per commit and per change barely differ (76.6 against 77.0 days). The unit matters more where pull requests are large.

Go is Low in every release-based definition, from 69.1 days (to the first tag, beta and rc included) to 138.0 days (to the final release, backports left out). It ships a feature release every six months, and in our window go1.26.0 was tagged on 10 February 2026 and go1.27.0 on 19 August 2026. Count a landing on master as the deploy and the same project is High, at 4.1 days.

The other repos show smaller spreads. Prometheus is Low (31.9 to 40.5 days) in every release-based definition. uv releases about twice a week, so it is High (28.6 to 30.1 hours) in all of them.

## The merge button decides what Git remembers

This is the part we did not expect to be so clean. With the default behaviour of each button, the way a change lands rewrites its timestamps, and the lead time follows. `demo-date-rewrite.sh` writes two commits on Monday and Wednesday, lands them on Friday three ways, and reads the result back:

```terminal
{
  "title": "one change, three merge buttons",
  "prompt": "$",
  "autoplay": false,
  "steps": [
    { "cmd": "./scripts/demo-date-rewrite.sh", "output": "merge commit:\n  feature: part 2 | author 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00\n  feature: part 1 | author 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date 102.0 h, from committer date 102.0 h\n\nsquash:\n  feature (squashed) | author 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date   0.0 h, from committer date   0.0 h\n\nrebase:\n  feature: part 2 | author 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  feature: part 1 | author 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date 102.0 h, from committer date   0.0 h" }
  ]
}
```

A merge commit keeps the original commits and their dates. A squash writes one new commit dated at the landing. A rebase keeps the author dates but resets the committer dates. Git lets you override both dates, so these are defaults, not laws, and a commit date is not proof of when a commit was pushed.

The real repos behave the same way:

- **uv** lands every change as a single commit. In 3,090 of its 3,132 landings, the author date is within 60 seconds of the commit date, so "first commit to landing" is 0.0 hours at the median and at the 75th percentile. Git cannot tell how long a uv pull request was open.
- **Prometheus** uses both buttons. The 626 changes it landed with a merge commit took 48.2 hours from first commit to merge at the median. The 615 squashed or pushed ones show 0.0 hours, and in 612 of them the author date is within a minute of the commit date. Same project, same maintainers, same year.
- **Go** submits through Gerrit, which rebases but keeps the author date. None of its 3,414 single-commit landings has an author date within 60 seconds of the commit date, and the median from author date to submit is 99.0 hours (4.1 days).

So with these defaults, a team that squash-merges looks Elite on "first commit to landing" lead time however long its reviews take, and a team that switches from merge commits to squash improves that number overnight without changing anything else.

## Three rules for a failed deploy

Git has no incidents, so a Git-only tool has to infer failures. We tested three common rules:

| Repo       | Next patch within 7 days | Release ships any revert | A later release reverts a change it shipped |
| ---------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- |
| Kubernetes | 0 of 42 (0.0%)           | 4 of 42 (9.5%)           | 1 of 42 (2.4%)                              |
| uv         | 87 of 101 (86.1%)        | 20 of 101 (19.8%)        | 5 of 101 (5.0%)                             |
| Go         | 4 of 30 (13.3%)          | 2 of 30 (6.7%)           | 1 of 30 (3.3%)                              |
| Prometheus | 5 of 26 (19.2%)          | 7 of 26 (26.9%)          | 1 of 26 (3.8%)                              |

Each rule fails in its own way:

- **The hotfix rule reads a version scheme as failures.** uv increments the patch number for nearly every release and shipped 101 in the year, so the next patch almost always follows within a week: 86.1%. Kubernetes publishes patch releases monthly, so the same rule never fires: 0.0%. Neither number says anything about failures.
- **"Ships a revert" counts reverts that users never saw.** Many reverts undo a change before it reaches a release. In Kubernetes, 34 reverts that shipped in a final release undid a change that had not shipped in an earlier release in the window: either it was never released, or it shipped before the window.
- **"Reverts a shipped change" needs the revert to name its target in a form a script can read.** Our parser looks for "reverts commit <hash>" and "reverts CL <number>". It could not resolve 21 Kubernetes reverts and 19 uv reverts: their messages name no commit that way, sometimes none at all, so Git alone cannot tell what they undid. Go writes "This reverts CL 847405" instead of a commit hash, and the script resolves those through the `Reviewed-on` trailer that Gerrit adds. Your conventions decide what this rule can see.

The strict rule finds one failure each in Kubernetes, Go and Prometheus. With one event, the rate is noise: one more revert would double it.

Recovery time inherits all of this. Under the hotfix rule it is the time to the next patch: 3.0 days for uv, 5.2 for Prometheus, 6.0 for Go. It can never be longer than 7 days, because the rule only counts a failure when the fix came within 7 days. The number measures the rule, not the recovery.

## What a dashboard adds, and what it does not

The script shows what Git alone supports. Engineering-metrics tools such as LinearB, Swarmia, Jellyfish, DX, Sleuth and Faros AI connect to more systems than Git, and those systems hold data Git does not:

- **When the work started.** The forge API knows when a pull request was opened and when its first commit was pushed, which is exactly what a squash merge removes from Git.
- **Real deploy events.** A CD pipeline knows when a version reached production, per environment. A tag is a stand-in.
- **Real failures.** Linking deploys to incidents, rollbacks and pages measures failure directly instead of inferring it from reverts or patch numbers.
- **Ownership and trends.** Mapping repos and paths to services and teams, and recomputing the same way every week.

What a tool does not remove is the choice of definition. Before you compare a number across teams or tools, ask which event counts as a deploy, which timestamp starts lead time, whether backports and release candidates count, and what counts as a failure. If the answers differ, the numbers do not compare.

## What we could not conclude

- **These projects do not deploy.** Tags stand in for deploys. The results test the definitions, not the projects.
- **The levels are survey clusters.** The report presents them as groups of answers, and the band edges are our reading.
- **The failure numbers are small.** The strict revert rule found one failure in three of the four projects, so its rates move a lot with one release.
- **Unshipped work is left out.** Lead time counts only commits that reached a release before the window closed, so the medians describe completed work. Go's work since the go1.27 freeze is not in them.
- **Tag dates differ.** uv and Go use lightweight tags, dated by the tagged commit. Kubernetes and Prometheus use annotated tags, dated by the tagger. A release can be published hours after its tag.

## Run it on your repositories

```bash
git clone https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git.git
cd dora-from-git
./run.sh
cat results/summary.md
```

To measure your own repositories, edit `repos.json`. Each entry needs a clone URL, the default branch, and a regular expression for your release tags, with groups for major, minor and patch and an optional fourth group for prerelease suffixes:

```json
{
  "name": "payments-api",
  "url": "git@gitlab.example.com:platform/payments-api.git",
  "branch": "main",
  "merge_style": "squash",
  "tag_pattern": "^v(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)(-rc\\.\\d+)?$"
}
```

If your deploys are not tagged, have your pipeline tag each production deploy with a version that the pattern matches, for example `v1.42.0`, and the same scripts measure deploys instead of releases. The hotfix rule reads the patch number, so it only means something if a patch bump marks a fix.

```github
https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git
```

## Summary

- The same Git history put each of the four projects in two or three different DORA levels, depending only on the definition.
- What counts as a deploy moved deployment frequency by up to two levels, and every project is Elite if a landing on the default branch counts.
- Where the clock starts and stops moved Kubernetes lead time from 5.2 to 81.9 days on the same history.
- With default settings, squash merges and rebases rewrite the timestamps lead time depends on, so the merge button can change the metric without changing the work.
- Change failure rate inferred from Git measured version schemes and commit message habits more than failures.
- Write the definitions down, keep them fixed, record the unit (commit or change), and compare a team with its own past.

     3	excerpt: 'We computed deployment frequency, lead time, change failure rate and recovery time from one year of Kubernetes, uv, Go and Prometheus history, once per common definition. The definition alone moved every project across two or three DORA performance levels, and the merge button decides whether Git still knows when the work started.'

     5	  name: 'DevOps'

    12	  name: 'DevOps Daily Team'

    22	---
    23	
    24	Most DORA dashboards start from the same raw material: your Git history, your tags and your pipeline events. We wanted to know how much of the final number comes from that data and how much comes from the definitions the tool picks for you. So we computed the four DORA metrics for one year of four open-source projects, under each definition we see in scripts and tools, and placed every result in the performance levels of the 2024 DORA report.
    25	
    26	The definition alone moved every project across at least two levels. From the same Kubernetes commits, deployment frequency is **Medium** (42 final releases, 0.81 a week), **High** (63 tags with the alpha, beta and rc tags, 1.21 a week) or **Elite** (3,286 merges to master, 63 a week). Lead time is **High** (5.2 days) if a merge to master counts as the deploy, **Medium** (25.9 days) to the first tag, and **Low** (76.6 days) to the first final release. For uv, which squash-merges, the lead time from first commit to merge is 0.0 hours, because a squash commit keeps no record of when the work started.
    27	
    28	## TL;DR
    29	
    30	- **A deploy is a definition.** Every project lands in **Elite** for deployment frequency if each merge to the default branch counts, and in **Medium** or **High** if releases count.
    31	- **Lead time depends on where the clock starts and stops.** Kubernetes ranges from 5.2 days to 81.9 days on the same commits, depending on the start timestamp, the release that stops the clock, and whether backports count.
    32	- **The merge button erases history.** In 3,090 of uv's 3,132 landings, the author date is within a minute of the landing time. Prometheus uses both buttons: changes merged with a merge commit show 48.2 hours from first commit to merge, squashed changes show 0.0 hours.
    33	- **Change failure rate is the least stable of the four.** uv is at 87.1%, 19.8% or 5.0% depending on whether a failure is a quick patch release, any revert, or a revert of a change that shipped.
    34	- **What to do:** write the definitions down, keep them fixed, and compare a team with its own past, not with a level chart or another tool.
    35	
    36	## Prerequisites
    37	
    38	- `git` and `python3` (standard library only), plus `bc` for the merge-style demo
    39	- A repository whose tags mark releases, or a list of deploy events you can turn into tags
    40	- The companion repo [dora-from-git](https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git), which holds every script and every recorded result in this post
    41	
    42	## How we measured
    43	
    44	We took one year, 1 October 2025 to 1 October 2026, and four projects that merge in different ways:
    45	
    46	| Repo                            | How changes land                                                    | What marks a release                        |
    47	| ------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
    48	| kubernetes/kubernetes           | merge commits                                                       | `vX.Y.Z` tags, plus alpha, beta and rc tags |
    49	| astral-sh/uv                    | squash                                                              | `X.Y.Z` tags                                |
    50	| golang/go (go.googlesource.com) | rebase through Gerrit, linear history                               | `goX.Y.Z` tags, plus beta and rc tags       |
    51	| prometheus/prometheus           | mixed: 626 merge commits and 615 squashed or pushed commits on main | `vX.Y.Z` tags, plus rc tags                 |
    52	
    53	The scripts use plain Git and nothing else. Go is hosted on Gerrit, not GitHub, and the same code reads it. Treeless clones keep every commit and tag but no file contents, which is enough for metadata: the four repos took 134 MB and one full run took 56 seconds on a Raspberry Pi 5.
    54	
    55	The pipeline is four stages, each writing a file the next one reads:
    56	
    57	1. `00-clone.sh` clones each repo and records its head commit.
    58	2. `01-extract.py` records, for every tag in the window, the commits it shipped for the first time, every first-parent commit on the default branch with the commits it brought in, and every revert with the commit it names.
    59	3. `02-metrics.py` computes each metric under each definition.
    60	4. `03-tiers.py` places the results in the 2024 levels.
    61	
    62	Before we trusted the extract, `check-method.py` compared it with plain Git on seeded random samples. The commits each merge brought in matched `git rev-list --no-merges <first-parent>..<merge>` on 50 of 50 merges, and the first final release that shipped a commit matched `git tag --contains` on 45 of 45 commits.
    63	
    64	The levels come from the [2024 Accelerate State of DevOps report](https://dora.dev/research/2024/dora-report/) (version 2024.3, page 13):
    65	
    66	| Level  | Change lead time                 | Deployment frequency                             | Change fail rate | Failed deployment recovery time |
    67	| ------ | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------ | ---------------- | ------------------------------- |
    68	| Elite  | Less than one day                | On demand (multiple deploys per day)             | 5%               | Less than one hour              |
    69	| High   | Between one day and one week     | Between once per day and once per week           | 20%              | Less than one day               |
    70	| Medium | Between one week and one month   | Between once per week and once per month         | 10%              | Less than one day               |
    71	| Low    | Between one month and six months | Between once per month and once every six months | 40%              | Between one week and one month  |
    72	
    73	The report describes these as clusters of survey answers, not as thresholds. We use the ranges the way a dashboard does, as bands. We place only deployment frequency and lead time, because change fail rate is a single value per level and is not in order: Medium (10%) sits below High (20%).
    74	
    75	:::note
    76	These projects ship releases, not production deploys, so a tag stands in for a deploy. That makes them a fair test of the definitions, and an unfair basis for ranking the projects. Nothing here says one project is better run than another.
    77	:::
    78	
    79	## What counts as a deploy
    80	
    81	Four definitions, all in real use: every tag, every final release, every day with a final release, and every merge to the default branch. The last one is common in tools that read only the Git host, because a merge is the only event they can see.
    82	
    83	| Repo       | Every tag         | Final releases    | Release days      | Merges to default branch |
    84	| ---------- | ----------------- | ----------------- | ----------------- | ------------------------ |
    85	| Kubernetes | 1.21/week, High   | 0.81/week, Medium | 0.29/week, Medium | 63.0/week, Elite         |
    86	| uv         | 1.94/week, High   | 1.94/week, High   | 1.78/week, High   | 60.1/week, Elite         |
    87	| Go         | 0.69/week, Medium | 0.57/week, Medium | 0.29/week, Medium | 65.6/week, Elite         |
    88	| Prometheus | 0.75/week, Medium | 0.50/week, Medium | 0.46/week, Medium | 23.8/week, Elite         |
    89	
    90	Kubernetes shows how much the choice matters. It cut 42 final releases in the year, but only on 15 days, because it patches several release lines on the same day. Count tags, releases or release days and you get three different numbers from the same calendar. Count merges and every project is Elite, including Go, whose master branch is not a release.
    91	
    92	Your company has the same choice, with different names: every merge to main, every pipeline run, every rollout to production, or every release train. Pick one and write it down.
    93	
    94	## Where the lead time clock starts and stops
    95	
    96	Lead time has two ends, and Git offers several timestamps for each.
    97	
    98	```diagram
    99	{
   100	  "type": "flow",
   101	  "nodes": [
   102	    { "label": "First commit", "sub": "author date", "icon": "branch", "tone": "slate" },
   103	    { "label": "Last rebase or amend", "sub": "committer date", "icon": "gear", "tone": "slate" },
   104	    { "label": "Lands on main", "sub": "merge or submit", "icon": "check", "tone": "blue" },
   105	    { "label": "First tag", "sub": "alpha, beta or rc", "icon": "box", "tone": "amber" },
   106	    { "label": "Final release", "sub": "the deploy", "icon": "rocket", "tone": "green" }
   107	  ]
   108	}
   109	```
   110	
   111	Here is Kubernetes, on the same commits, seven ways:
   112	
   113	| Clock starts                           | Clock stops                            | Changes | Median | Level  |
   114	| -------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ------: | -----: | ------ |
   115	| first commit of the change             | merge to master, if that is the deploy |   3,286 |  5.2 d | High   |
   116	| commit author date                     | first tag, alpha to rc included        |   6,812 | 25.9 d | Medium |
   117	| landing on master                      | first final release                    |   3,734 | 60.6 d | Low    |
   118	| commit committer date                  | first final release                    |   6,091 | 63.1 d | Low    |
   119	| commit author date                     | first final release                    |   6,091 | 76.6 d | Low    |
   120	| first commit of the change             | first final release                    |   3,734 | 77.0 d | Low    |
   121	| commit author date, backports left out | first final release                    |   5,486 | 81.9 d | Low    |
   122	
   123	```chart
   124	{
   125	  "type": "bar",
   126	  "title": "Kubernetes change lead time, same commits, seven definitions",
   127	  "unit": " days",
   128	  "caption": "Median days. 1 Oct 2025 to 1 Oct 2026. Source: results/summary.md in The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git.",
   129	  "rows": [
   130	    { "label": "Merge is the deploy", "value": 5.2, "series": "High" },
   131	    { "label": "To first tag (alpha to rc)", "value": 25.9, "series": "Medium" },
   132	    { "label": "Landing to final release", "value": 60.6, "series": "Low" },
   133	    { "label": "Committer date to final", "value": 63.1, "series": "Low" },
   134	    { "label": "Author date to final", "value": 76.6, "series": "Low" },
   135	    { "label": "First commit of change to final", "value": 77.0, "series": "Low" },
   136	    { "label": "Author date, no backports", "value": 81.9, "series": "Low" }
   137	  ],
   138	  "series": [
   139	    { "name": "High", "color": "#10b981" },
   140	    { "name": "Medium", "color": "#f59e0b" },
   141	    { "name": "Low", "color": "#ef4444" }
   142	  ]
   143	}
   144	```
   145	
   146	Four choices do most of the moving:
   147	
   148	- **Which release stops the clock.** Kubernetes cuts alpha tags from master, so a commit reaches its first tag 25.9 days after it is written, and its first final release 76.6 days after. If your release candidates never serve users, they are not deploys.
   149	- **Author date or committer date.** The committer date changes every time a commit is rebased or amended, the author date does not. On Kubernetes, switching to the committer date moves the median from 76.6 to 63.1 days.
   150	- **Backports.** 605 of the 6,091 commits that Kubernetes final releases shipped never landed on master. They were made on release branches: mostly cherry-picked fixes, plus the release commits themselves. `git cherry-pick` keeps the original author date, so a cherry-pick counts the time since the fix was first written. Leaving these commits out moves the median from 76.6 to 81.9 days.
   151	- **Per commit or per change.** A pull request with ten commits counts ten times per commit and once per change. On Kubernetes this barely matters (76.6 against 77.0 days). It matters more where pull requests are large.
   152	
   153	Go is Low in every release-based definition, from 69.1 days (to the first rc) to 138.0 days (to the final release, backports left out). It ships a feature release every six months, and in our window go1.26.0 was tagged on 10 February 2026 and go1.27.0 on 19 August 2026. Count a submit to master as the deploy and the same project is High, at 4.1 days.
   154	
   155	The other repos show smaller spreads. Prometheus is Low (31.9 to 40.5 days) in every release-based definition. uv releases about twice a week, so it is High (28.6 to 30.1 hours) in all of them.
   156	
   157	## The merge button decides what Git remembers
   158	
   159	This is the part we did not expect to be so clean. The way a change lands rewrites its timestamps, and the lead time follows. `demo-date-rewrite.sh` writes two commits on Monday and Wednesday, lands them on Friday three ways, and reads the result back:
   160	
   161	```terminal
   162	{
   163	  "title": "one change, three merge buttons",
   164	  "prompt": "$",
   165	  "autoplay": false,
   166	  "steps": [
   167	    { "cmd": "./scripts/demo-date-rewrite.sh", "output": "merge commit:\n  feature: part 2 | author 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00\n  feature: part 1 | author 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date 102.0 h, from committer date 102.0 h\n\nsquash:\n  feature (squashed) | author 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date   0.0 h, from committer date   0.0 h\n\nrebase:\n  feature: part 2 | author 2026-09-09T15:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  feature: part 1 | author 2026-09-07T10:00:00+00:00 | committer 2026-09-11T16:00:00+00:00\n  lead time from author date 102.0 h, from committer date   0.0 h" }
   168	  ]
   169	}
   170	```
   171	
   172	A merge commit keeps the original commits and their dates. A squash writes one new commit dated at the landing. A rebase keeps the author dates but resets the committer dates to the landing.
   173	
   174	The real repos behave the same way:
   175	
   176	- **uv** lands every change as a single commit, almost always by squash. In 3,090 of its 3,132 landings, the author date is within 60 seconds of the commit date, so "first commit to merge" is 0.0 hours at the median and at the 75th percentile. Git cannot tell how long a uv pull request was open.
   177	- **Prometheus** uses both buttons. The 626 changes it landed with a merge commit took 48.2 hours from first commit to merge at the median. The 615 squashed or pushed ones show 0.0 hours, and in 612 of them the author date is within a minute of the commit date. Same project, same maintainers, same year.
   178	- **Go** submits through Gerrit, which rebases but keeps the author date. None of its 3,414 single-commit landings has an author date within 60 seconds of the commit date, and the median from author date to submit is 99.0 hours (4.1 days).
   179	
   180	So a team that squash-merges looks Elite on "first commit to merge" lead time however long its reviews take, and a team that switches from merge commits to squash improves its lead time overnight without changing anything else.
   181	
   182	## Three rules for a failed deploy
   183	
   184	Git has no incidents, so a Git-only tool has to infer failures. These are the three rules we see most often:
   185	
   186	| Repo       | Next patch within 7 days | Release ships any revert | A later release reverts a change it shipped |
   187	| ---------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- |
   188	| Kubernetes | 0 of 42 (0.0%)           | 4 of 42 (9.5%)           | 1 of 42 (2.4%)                              |
   189	| uv         | 88 of 101 (87.1%)        | 20 of 101 (19.8%)        | 5 of 101 (5.0%)                             |
   190	| Go         | 4 of 30 (13.3%)          | 2 of 30 (6.7%)           | 1 of 30 (3.3%)                              |
   191	| Prometheus | 5 of 26 (19.2%)          | 7 of 26 (26.9%)          | 1 of 26 (3.8%)                              |
   192	
   193	Each rule fails in its own way:
   194	
   195	- **The hotfix rule reads a version scheme as failures.** uv increments the patch number for every release and shipped 101 in the year, so the next patch almost always follows within a week: 87.1%. Kubernetes publishes patch releases monthly, so the same rule never fires: 0.0%. Neither number says anything about failures.
   196	- **"Ships a revert" counts reverts that users never saw.** Many reverts undo a change before it reaches a release. In Kubernetes, 34 reverts that shipped in a final release undid a change that had not shipped in an earlier release in the window: either it was never released, or it shipped before the window.
   197	- **"Reverts a shipped change" needs the revert to name its target.** 25 Kubernetes reverts and 22 uv reverts name no commit in their message, so Git alone cannot tell what they undid. Go writes "This reverts CL 847405" instead of a commit hash, and the script resolves those through the `Reviewed-on` trailer that Gerrit adds. Your conventions decide what this rule can see.
   198	
   199	The strict rule finds one failure each in Kubernetes, Go and Prometheus. With one event, the rate is noise: one more revert would double it.
   200	
   201	Recovery time inherits all of this. Under the hotfix rule it is the time to the next patch: 2.9 days for uv, 5.2 for Prometheus, 6.0 for Go. It can never be longer than 7 days, because the rule only counts a failure when the fix came within 7 days. The number measures the rule, not the recovery.
   202	
   203	## What a dashboard adds, and what it does not
   204	
   205	The script shows what Git alone supports. A commercial engineering-metrics tool such as LinearB, Swarmia, Jellyfish, DX, Sleuth or Faros AI adds real data on top:
   206	
   207	- **When the work started.** The forge API knows when a pull request was opened and when its first commit was pushed, which is exactly what a squash merge removes from Git.
   208	- **Real deploy events.** A CD pipeline knows when a version reached production, per environment. A tag is a stand-in.
   209	- **Real failures.** Linking deploys to incidents, rollbacks and pages measures failure directly instead of inferring it from reverts or patch numbers.
   210	- **Ownership and trends.** Mapping repos and paths to services and teams, and recomputing the same way every week.
   211	
   212	What a tool does not remove is the choice of definition. Before you compare a number across teams or tools, ask which event counts as a deploy, which timestamp starts lead time, whether backports and release candidates count, and what counts as a failure. If the answers differ, the numbers do not compare.
   213	
   214	## What we could not conclude
   215	
   216	- **These projects do not deploy.** Tags stand in for deploys. The results test the definitions, not the projects.
   217	- **The levels are survey clusters.** The report presents them as groups of answers, and the band edges are our reading.
   218	- **The failure numbers are small.** The strict revert rule found one failure in three of the four projects, so its rates move a lot with one release.
   219	- **Unshipped work is left out.** Lead time counts only commits that reached a release before the window closed. Go's work since the go1.27 freeze is missing, which pulls its medians down.
   220	- **Tag dates differ.** uv and Go use lightweight tags, dated by the tagged commit. Kubernetes and Prometheus use annotated tags, dated by the tagger. A release can be published hours after its tag.
   221	
   222	## Run it on your repositories
   223	
   224	```bash
   225	git clone https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git.git
   226	cd dora-from-git
   227	./run.sh
   228	cat results/summary.md
   229	```
   230	
   231	To measure your own repositories, edit `repos.json`. Each entry needs a clone URL, the default branch, and a regular expression for your release tags, with groups for major, minor and patch and an optional fourth group for prerelease suffixes:
   232	
   233	```json
   234	{
   235	  "name": "payments-api",
   236	  "url": "git@gitlab.example.com:platform/payments-api.git",
   237	  "branch": "main",
   238	  "merge_style": "squash",
   239	  "tag_pattern": "^v(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)(-rc\\.\\d+)?$"
   240	}
   241	```
   242	
   243	If your deploys are not tagged, tag them from your pipeline (`git tag deploy-2026-10-14-1 <sha>`) and the same scripts measure deploys instead of releases.
   244	
   245	```github
   246	https://github.com/The-DevOps-Daily/dora-from-git
   247	```
   248	
   249	## Summary
   250	
   251	- The same Git history put each of the four projects in two or three different DORA levels, depending only on the definition.
   252	- What counts as a deploy moved deployment frequency by up to two levels, and every project is Elite if a merge counts.
   253	- Where the clock starts and stops moved Kubernetes lead time from 5.2 to 81.9 days on the same commits.
   254	- Squash merges and rebases rewrite the timestamps lead time depends on, so the merge button changes the metric without changing the work.
   255	- Change failure rate inferred from Git measured version schemes and commit message habits more than failures.
   256	- Write the definitions down, keep them fixed, record the unit (commit or change), and compare a team with its own past.

     4	./run.sh
     5	```
     6	
     7	Compute the four DORA metrics (deployment frequency, change lead time, change failure rate, recovery time) from plain Git history, once per definition, and see how far the definition alone moves the result. It needs `git`, `python3` (standard library only) and `bc`. A run took 56 seconds on a Raspberry Pi 5, and the treeless clones of the four repos take 134 MB.
     8	
     9	The repos it measures, one per merge style, over 1 October 2025 to 1 October 2026:
    10	
    11	| Repo | Merge style | Deploy record |
    12	| --- | --- | --- |
    13	| kubernetes/kubernetes | merge commits | `vX.Y.Z` tags, alpha, beta and rc tags |
    14	| astral-sh/uv | squash | `X.Y.Z` tags |
    15	| golang/go (go.googlesource.com) | rebase through Gerrit, linear history | `goX.Y.Z` tags, beta and rc tags |
    16	| prometheus/prometheus | mixed: about half merge commits, half squash | `vX.Y.Z` tags and rc tags |
    17	
    18	Edit `repos.json` to point it at your own repositories. Any Git host works: nothing here calls a forge API.
    19	
    20	## What each script does
    21	
    22	| Script | Reads | Writes |
    23	| --- | --- | --- |
    24	| `scripts/00-clone.sh` | `repos.json` | treeless clones in `data/repos/` (commits and tags, no file contents), `results/clones.json` with the head of each |
    25	| `scripts/01-extract.py` | the clones | `results/extract/<repo>.json.gz`: tags and the commits each one shipped, every first-parent commit on the default branch with the commits it brought in, and every revert with the commit or Gerrit change it names |
    26	| `scripts/02-metrics.py` | the extracts | `results/metrics.json`: every metric under every definition |
    27	| `scripts/03-tiers.py` | `results/metrics.json` | `results/tiers.json` and `results/summary.md`: each result placed in the 2024 DORA performance levels |
    28	| `scripts/demo-date-rewrite.sh` | nothing | `results/demo-date-rewrite.txt`: one change landed three ways (merge commit, squash, rebase) and the lead time each one leaves in Git |
    29	| `scripts/check-method.py` | the extracts and the clones | `results/method-checks.txt`: stage 1 cross-checked against plain git on seeded random samples |
    30	
    31	Each stage stamps its output with a hash of its own code and inputs. `02-metrics.py` refuses to mix extracts from different extract code.
    32	
    33	## The definitions
    34	
    35	- **Deploy:** every tag, release candidates included; every final release tag; days with a final release; or every first-parent commit on the default branch ("merge to main is a deploy").
    36	- **Lead time:** from the commit author date, the committer date, the time the change landed on the default branch, or the first commit of the change; to the first tag or to the first final release that contains it. Per commit or per change (one landing on the default branch).
    37	- **Failure:** the next patch on the same `major.minor` line came within 7 days; the release ships any commit whose subject starts with "Revert"; or a later release reverts a change this release shipped.
    38	- **Recovery:** from the failed release to the release that fixed it, for the two failure rules that name one.
    39	
    40	The performance levels come from the 2024 Accelerate State of DevOps report (v. 2024.3, page 13). They describe survey clusters, not official thresholds, and the report gives single values for change failure rate (5%, 20%, 10%, 40%), so `03-tiers.py` only places deployment frequency and lead time.
    41	
    42	## Results
    43	
    44	See `results/summary.md` for every number, and the post for what they mean. In short: each repo reaches two or three different levels for the same metric depending on the definition, and the merge button decides whether Git still knows when the work started.
    45	
    46	## Checks done on the method
    47	
    48	`scripts/check-method.py` (seeded) compares stage 1 with plain git: the side commits each merge brought in against `git rev-list --no-merges <first-parent>..<merge>` on 50 random merges, and the first final release that ships a commit against `git tag --contains` on 45 random commits. All 95 match; see `results/method-checks.txt`.

     8	      "name": "kubernetes",
     9	      "url": "https://github.com/kubernetes/kubernetes.git",
    10	      "branch": "master",
    11	      "merge_style": "merge commits",
    12	      "tag_pattern": "^v(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)\\.(\\d+)(-(?:alpha|beta|rc)\\.\\d+)?$"
    13	    },
    14	    {
    15	      "name": "uv",
    16	      "url": "https://github.com/astral-sh/uv.git",

     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Stage 1: read commit metadata and tags from each clone into results/extract/<repo>.json.
     3	
     4	Only plain git is used, so it works for any host. Nothing here decides what a
     5	deploy or a failure is; stage 2 does that from this file.
     6	"""
     7	import gzip
     8	import hashlib
     9	import json
    10	import re
    11	import subprocess
    12	import sys
    13	from datetime import datetime, timezone
    14	from pathlib import Path
    15	
    16	ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
    17	CONFIG = json.loads((ROOT / "repos.json").read_text())
    18	OUT = ROOT / "results" / "extract"
    19	SEP = "\x1f"
    20	YEAR = 365 * 86400
    21	
    22	
    23	def git(repo, *args):
    24	    return subprocess.run(["git", "-C", str(repo), *args], check=True, capture_output=True, text=True).stdout
    25	
    26	
    27	def iso_to_ts(value):
    28	    return int(datetime.fromisoformat(value).timestamp())
    29	
    30	
    31	def code_hash():
    32	    h = hashlib.sha256()
    33	    for f in (Path(__file__), ROOT / "repos.json", ROOT / "results" / "clones.json"):
    34	        h.update(f.read_bytes())
    35	    return h.hexdigest()[:16]
    36	
    37	
    38	def read_tags(repo, pattern):
    39	    fmt = SEP.join(["%(refname:short)", "%(objecttype)", "%(objectname)", "%(*objectname)", "%(creatordate:iso-strict)"])
    40	    rx = re.compile(pattern)
    41	    tags = []
    42	    for line in git(repo, "for-each-ref", "refs/tags", f"--format={fmt}").splitlines():
    43	        name, otype, obj, peeled, created = line.split(SEP)
    44	        m = rx.match(name)
    45	        if not m:
    46	            continue
    47	        nums = [int(g) if g else 0 for g in m.groups()[:3]]
    48	        tags.append({
    49	            "name": name,
    50	            "annotated": otype == "tag",
    51	            "commit": peeled if otype == "tag" else obj,
    52	            # Annotated: the tagger date. Lightweight: the tagged commit's committer date.
    53	            "ts": iso_to_ts(created),
    54	            "major": nums[0], "minor": nums[1], "patch": nums[2],
    55	            "prerelease": bool(m.groups()[3]),
    56	        })
    57	    tags.sort(key=lambda t: (t["ts"], t["name"]))
    58	    return tags
    59	
    60	
    61	def rev_list(repo, *args):
    62	    """Commits as dicts: sha, parents, author ts, committer ts, subject."""
    63	    fmt = SEP.join(["%H", "%P", "%at", "%ct", "%s"])
    64	    rows = []
    65	    for line in git(repo, "log", f"--format={fmt}", *args).splitlines():
    66	        sha, parents, at, ct, subject = line.split(SEP, 4)
    67	        rows.append({"sha": sha, "parents": parents.split(), "at": int(at), "ct": int(ct), "subject": subject})
    68	    return rows
    69	
    70	
    71	def shipped(repo, tag, earlier):
    72	    """Commits reachable from tag and from none of the earlier tags."""
    73	    if earlier:
    74	        return rev_list(repo, tag["commit"], "--not", *[t["commit"] for t in earlier])
    75	    return rev_list(repo, tag["commit"])
    76	
    77	
    78	def landings_with_side_commits(repo, branch, since, until):
    79	    """First-parent commits on the default branch, oldest first, each with the
    80	    commits it brought in. A merge brings the commits of its second parent that
    81	    no earlier landing brought; other commits bring only themselves."""
    82	    graph = {c["sha"]: c for c in rev_list(repo, branch, f"--since={since - YEAR}")}
    83	    firsts = rev_list(repo, "--first-parent", "--reverse", branch, f"--since={since}", f"--until={until}")
    84	    mainline = {m["sha"] for m in firsts}
    85	    assigned = set()
    86	    out = []
    87	    for m in firsts:
    88	        brought = []
    89	        if len(m["parents"]) > 1:
    90	            stack = list(m["parents"][1:])
    91	            while stack:
    92	                sha = stack.pop()
    93	                if sha in assigned or sha in mainline or sha not in graph:
    94	                    continue
    95	                assigned.add(sha)
    96	                c = graph[sha]
    97	                if len(c["parents"]) == 1:
    98	                    brought.append({"sha": sha, "at": c["at"], "ct": c["ct"]})
    99	                stack.extend(c["parents"])
   100	        out.append({**m, "brought": brought})
   101	    return out
   102	
   103	
   104	def read_reverts(repo, since):
   105	    fmt = SEP.join(["%H", "%at", "%ct", "%s", "%b"]) + "\x1e"
   106	    reverts = []
   107	    for rec in git(repo, "log", "--all", "-i", "--grep=^Revert", f"--since={since}", f"--format={fmt}").split("\x1e"):
   108	        rec = rec.strip("\n")
   109	        if not rec:
   110	            continue
   111	        sha, at, ct, subject, body = rec.split(SEP, 4)
   112	        target_sha = re.search(r"reverts commit ([0-9a-f]{7,40})", body)
   113	        target_cl = re.search(r"reverts CL (\d+)", body)
   114	        reverts.append({
   115	            "sha": sha, "at": int(at), "ct": int(ct), "subject": subject,
   116	            "target_sha": target_sha.group(1) if target_sha else None,
   117	            "target_cl": target_cl.group(1) if target_cl else None,
   118	        })
   119	    return reverts
   120	
   121	
   122	def gerrit_change_map(repo, since, wanted):
   123	    """Gerrit only: change number -> commit, from the Reviewed-on trailer."""
   124	    out = git(repo, "log", "--all", f"--since={since}",
   125	              "--format=%H %(trailers:key=Reviewed-on,valueonly,separator=%x20)")
   126	    found = {}
   127	    for line in out.splitlines():
   128	        parts = line.split()
   129	        if len(parts) >= 2:
   130	            m = re.search(r"/\+/(\d+)", parts[1])
   131	            if m and m.group(1) in wanted:
   132	                found.setdefault(m.group(1), parts[0])
   133	    return found
   134	
   135	
   136	def main():
   137	    start = iso_to_ts(CONFIG["window"]["start"])
   138	    end = iso_to_ts(CONFIG["window"]["end"])
   139	    OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
   140	    clones = json.loads((ROOT / "results" / "clones.json").read_text())
   141	    for r in CONFIG["repos"]:
   142	        repo = ROOT / "data" / "repos" / r["name"]
   143	        branch = f"origin/{r['branch']}"
   144	        tags = read_tags(repo, r["tag_pattern"])
   145	
   146	        # A final release also gets the set measured against earlier final
   147	        # releases only, because a release candidate is not a production deploy.
   148	        deploys = []
   149	        for i, t in enumerate(tags):
   150	            if not (start <= t["ts"] < end):
   151	                continue
   152	            entry = {**t, "commits": shipped(repo, t, tags[:i])}
   153	            if not t["prerelease"]:
   154	                entry["commits_vs_finals"] = shipped(repo, t, [p for p in tags[:i] if not p["prerelease"]])
   155	            deploys.append(entry)
   156	
   157	        # Landings from a year before the window: stage 2 needs them to know when
   158	        # a change shipped early in the window first landed.
   159	        landings = landings_with_side_commits(repo, branch, start - YEAR, end)
   160	        reverts = read_reverts(repo, start - YEAR)
   161	        wanted = {rv["target_cl"] for rv in reverts if rv["target_cl"]}
   162	        cl_map = gerrit_change_map(repo, start - 2 * YEAR, wanted) if wanted else {}
   163	
   164	        extract = {
   165	            "repo": r["name"],
   166	            "merge_style": r["merge_style"],
   167	            "head": clones[r["name"]]["head"],
   168	            "window": CONFIG["window"],
   169	            "code_hash": code_hash(),
   170	            "generated_at": datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(timespec="seconds"),
   171	            "all_tags": tags,
   172	            "deploys": deploys,
   173	            "landings": landings,
   174	            "reverts": reverts,
   175	            "cl_map": cl_map,
   176	        }
   177	        path = OUT / f"{r['name']}.json.gz"
   178	        with gzip.open(path, "wt") as f:
   179	            json.dump(extract, f)
   180	        in_window = sum(start <= m["ct"] < end for m in landings)
   181	        print(f"{r['name']}: {len(deploys)} tags in window ({sum(not d['prerelease'] for d in deploys)} final), "
   182	              f"{in_window} landings on {r['branch']}, {len(reverts)} reverts since a year before -> "
   183	              f"{path.relative_to(ROOT)}")
   184	    return 0
   185	
   186	
   187	if __name__ == "__main__":
   188	    sys.exit(main())

     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Stage 2: compute the four DORA metrics from results/extract/*.json, once per definition.
     3	
     4	Each metric has several defensible definitions. This script computes all of them
     5	side by side, so the spread is visible. Output: results/metrics.json.
     6	"""
     7	import gzip
     8	import hashlib
     9	import json
    10	import statistics
    11	import sys
    12	from datetime import datetime, timezone
    13	from pathlib import Path
    14	
    15	ROOT = Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent
    16	EXTRACT = ROOT / "results" / "extract"
    17	HOUR = 3600
    18	DAY = 86400
    19	HOTFIX_DAYS = 7  # a release followed this soon by the next patch on the same line counts as failed
    20	
    21	
    22	def iso_to_ts(value):
    23	    return int(datetime.fromisoformat(value).timestamp())
    24	
    25	
    26	def pct(values, q):
    27	    """Nearest-rank percentile; values need not be sorted."""
    28	    if not values:
    29	        return None
    30	    s = sorted(values)
    31	    k = max(0, min(len(s) - 1, int(round(q / 100 * len(s) + 0.5)) - 1))
    32	    return s[k]
    33	
    34	
    35	def summary(values_seconds):
    36	    if not values_seconds:
    37	        return {"n": 0, "median_h": None, "p75_h": None}
    38	    return {
    39	        "n": len(values_seconds),
    40	        "median_h": round(statistics.median(values_seconds) / HOUR, 2),
    41	        "p75_h": round(pct(values_seconds, 75) / HOUR, 2),
    42	    }
    43	
    44	
    45	def metrics_for(ex):
    46	    start = iso_to_ts(ex["window"]["start"])
    47	    end = iso_to_ts(ex["window"]["end"])
    48	    weeks = (end - start) / (7 * DAY)
    49	
    50	    deploys = ex["deploys"]
    51	    finals = [d for d in deploys if not d["prerelease"]]
    52	    landings_all = ex["landings"]
    53	    landings = [m for m in landings_all if start <= m["ct"] < end]
    54	
    55	    # When each commit first landed on the default branch, and the landing it came with.
    56	    landed = {}
    57	    for m in landings_all:
    58	        landed.setdefault(m["sha"], m)
    59	        for c in m["brought"]:
    60	            landed.setdefault(c["sha"], m)
    61	
    62	    # ---- Deployment frequency -------------------------------------------------
    63	    release_days = {datetime.fromtimestamp(d["ts"], timezone.utc).date() for d in finals}
    64	    df = {
    65	        "every_tag": len(deploys),
    66	        "final_tags": len(finals),
    67	        "release_days": len(release_days),
    68	        "default_branch_landings": len(landings),
    69	    }
    70	    df = {k: {"count": v, "per_week": round(v / weeks, 3)} for k, v in df.items()}
    71	
    72	    # ---- Change lead time (deploy = final release tag) ------------------------
    73	    per_commit_author, per_commit_committer, per_commit_author_no_backports = [], [], []
    74	    per_landing_from_landing, per_landing_from_first_commit = {}, {}
    75	    backports = 0
    76	    for d in finals:
    77	        for c in d["commits_vs_finals"]:
    78	            if len(c["parents"]) > 1:
    79	                continue  # merge commits carry no change of their own
    80	            per_commit_author.append(d["ts"] - c["at"])
    81	            per_commit_committer.append(d["ts"] - c["ct"])
    82	            m = landed.get(c["sha"])
    83	            if m is not None:
    84	                per_commit_author_no_backports.append(d["ts"] - c["at"])
    85	            if m is None:
    86	                # Never landed on the default branch: a backport or a release-branch commit.
    87	                backports += 1
    88	                key, land_ts, first_at = c["sha"], c["ct"], c["at"]
    89	            else:
    90	                key = m["sha"]
    91	                land_ts = m["ct"]
    92	                first_at = min([x["at"] for x in m["brought"]] or [m["at"]])
    93	            # One value per landing (pull request or change), at its first release.
    94	            if key not in per_landing_from_landing:
    95	                per_landing_from_landing[key] = d["ts"] - land_ts
    96	                per_landing_from_first_commit[key] = d["ts"] - first_at
    97	
    98	    # Lead time if every tag counted, release candidates included: each commit at
    99	    # the first tag that contained it.
   100	    first_tag_author = [d["ts"] - c["at"] for d in deploys for c in d["commits"] if len(c["parents"]) == 1]
   101	
   102	    # Lead time if every landing on the default branch counted as a deploy.
   103	    landing_as_deploy, landing_merge, landing_single = [], [], []
   104	    for m in landings:
   105	        first_at = min([x["at"] for x in m["brought"]] or [m["at"]])
   106	        landing_as_deploy.append(m["ct"] - first_at)
   107	        (landing_merge if len(m["parents"]) > 1 else landing_single).append(m["ct"] - first_at)
   108	
   109	    lt = {
   110	        "commit_author_date_first_tag": summary(first_tag_author),
   111	        "commit_author_date": summary(per_commit_author),
   112	        "commit_committer_date": summary(per_commit_committer),
   113	        "commit_author_date_without_backports": summary(per_commit_author_no_backports),
   114	        "change_from_landing": summary(list(per_landing_from_landing.values())),
   115	        "change_from_first_commit": summary(list(per_landing_from_first_commit.values())),
   116	        "landing_is_deploy": summary(landing_as_deploy),
   117	    }
   118	    # The same measure split by how the change landed: a merge commit keeps the
   119	    # side branch and its dates, a squash or rebase writes one new commit.
   120	    landing_split = {
   121	        "merge_commit_landings": summary(landing_merge),
   122	        "single_commit_landings": summary(landing_single),
   123	        # Squash and direct pushes: the author date is the landing time, to the minute.
   124	        "single_commit_landings_author_within_60s_of_committer": sum(
   125	            1 for m in landings if len(m["parents"]) == 1 and abs(m["ct"] - m["at"]) < 60),
   126	    }
   127	
   128	    # ---- Change failure rate and recovery (deploy = final release tag) --------
   129	    # 1. Hotfix: the next release on the same major.minor line came within HOTFIX_DAYS.
   130	    by_line = {}
   131	    for t in ex["all_tags"]:
   132	        if not t["prerelease"]:
   133	            by_line.setdefault((t["major"], t["minor"]), []).append(t)
   134	    hotfix_failed, hotfix_recovery = [], []
   135	    for d in finals:
   136	        line = sorted(by_line[(d["major"], d["minor"])], key=lambda t: (t["ts"], t["patch"]))
   137	        later = [t for t in line if t["patch"] > d["patch"] and t["ts"] >= d["ts"]]
   138	        if later and later[0]["ts"] - d["ts"] <= HOTFIX_DAYS * DAY:
   139	            hotfix_failed.append(d["name"])
   140	            hotfix_recovery.append(later[0]["ts"] - d["ts"])
   141	
   142	    # 2. The release ships at least one revert commit.
   143	    has_revert = [d["name"] for d in finals
   144	                  if any(c["subject"].lower().startswith("revert") for c in d["commits_vs_finals"])]
   145	
   146	    # 3. A later release reverts a change this release shipped.
   147	    shipped_in = {}
   148	    for d in sorted(finals, key=lambda x: x["ts"]):
   149	        for c in d["commits_vs_finals"]:
   150	            shipped_in.setdefault(c["sha"], d)
   151	    revert_shas = {rv["sha"]: rv for rv in ex["reverts"]}
   152	
   153	    def resolve(rv):
   154	        if rv["target_sha"]:
   155	            for sha in shipped_in:
   156	                if sha.startswith(rv["target_sha"]):
   157	                    return sha
   158	            return None
   159	        if rv["target_cl"]:
   160	            return ex["cl_map"].get(rv["target_cl"])
   161	        return None
   162	
   163	    reverted_failed, reverted_recovery = {}, []
   164	    no_target, target_not_in_earlier_release = 0, 0
   165	    for d in finals:
   166	        for c in d["commits_vs_finals"]:
   167	            rv = revert_shas.get(c["sha"])
   168	            if not rv or len(c["parents"]) > 1:
   169	                continue  # a merge commit that only quotes a revert in its message
   170	            if not rv["target_sha"] and not rv["target_cl"]:
   171	                no_target += 1
   172	                continue
   173	            target = resolve(rv)
   174	            if target is None or target not in shipped_in or shipped_in[target]["ts"] >= d["ts"]:
   175	                # Reverted before it reached a release in the window, or shipped earlier.
   176	                target_not_in_earlier_release += 1
   177	                continue
   178	            origin = shipped_in.get(target)
   179	            if origin is None or origin["ts"] >= d["ts"] or origin["name"] in reverted_failed:
   180	                continue
   181	            reverted_failed[origin["name"]] = d["name"]
   182	            reverted_recovery.append(d["ts"] - origin["ts"])
   183	
   184	    n = len(finals)
   185	    cfr = {
   186	        "hotfix_within_7_days": {"failed": len(hotfix_failed), "of": n,
   187	                                 "rate": round(len(hotfix_failed) / n, 3) if n else None},
   188	        "ships_a_revert": {"failed": len(has_revert), "of": n,
   189	                           "rate": round(len(has_revert) / n, 3) if n else None},
   190	        "shipped_change_later_reverted": {"failed": len(reverted_failed), "of": n,
   191	                                          "rate": round(len(reverted_failed) / n, 3) if n else None,
   192	                                          "reverts_naming_no_commit": no_target,
   193	                                          "reverts_of_unreleased_or_older_changes": target_not_in_earlier_release},
   194	    }
   195	    recovery = {
   196	        "hotfix_within_7_days": summary(hotfix_recovery),
   197	        "shipped_change_later_reverted": summary(reverted_recovery),
   198	    }
   199	
   200	    return {
   201	        "repo": ex["repo"],
   202	        "merge_style": ex["merge_style"],
   203	        "head": ex["head"],
   204	        "code_hash": ex["code_hash"],
   205	        "weeks": round(weeks, 2),
   206	        "deployment_frequency": df,
   207	        "lead_time": lt,
   208	        "landing_is_deploy_by_style": landing_split,
   209	        "backport_commits": backports,
   210	        "change_failure_rate": cfr,
   211	        "recovery_time": recovery,
   212	        "details": {
   213	            "hotfix_failed": hotfix_failed,
   214	            "ships_a_revert": has_revert,
   215	            "reverted": reverted_failed,
   216	        },
   217	    }
   218	
   219	
   220	def main():
   221	    results = []
   222	    hashes = set()
   223	    for path in sorted(EXTRACT.glob("*.json.gz")):
   224	        with gzip.open(path, "rt") as f:
   225	            ex = json.load(f)
   226	        hashes.add(ex["code_hash"])
   227	        results.append(metrics_for(ex))
   228	    if len(hashes) != 1:
   229	        print(f"refusing: extracts come from different code versions {hashes}", file=sys.stderr)
   230	        return 1
   231	    out = {"generated_at": datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(timespec="seconds"),
   232	           "extract_code_hash": hashes.pop(),
   233	           "metrics_code_hash": hashlib.sha256(Path(__file__).read_bytes()).hexdigest()[:16],
   234	           "hotfix_days": HOTFIX_DAYS, "repos": results}
   235	    (ROOT / "results" / "metrics.json").write_text(json.dumps(out, indent=2) + "\n")
   236	    for r in results:
   237	        print(f"== {r['repo']} ({r['merge_style']})")
   238	        print("  deploys/week:", {k: v["per_week"] for k, v in r["deployment_frequency"].items()})
   239	        print("  lead time median h:", {k: v["median_h"] for k, v in r["lead_time"].items()})
   240	        print("  CFR:", {k: v["rate"] for k, v in r["change_failure_rate"].items()})
   241	        print("  recovery median h:", {k: v["median_h"] for k, v in r["recovery_time"].items()})
   242	    return 0
   243	
   244	
   245	if __name__ == "__main__":
   246	    sys.exit(main())
